(65)

We can see that
(v_{3}\not\perp^{d}v_{4}|V_{\textup{post}})_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}
as
V_{\textup{post}}:=\{T_{1}\}
acts as an unblocking collider. It is easy to see that every member of
\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
will have this
d
-connection relative to its
V_{\textup{post}}
, as each such member will necessarily include at least one of the loop vertices
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
in
V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})
and hence always introduce at least one unblocking collider between
v_{3}
and
v_{4}
. Therefore, by
definition

19
, we have that
(v_{3}\not\perp^{p}v_{4})_{G}
. This
p
-connection explains the correlation between the outcomes of
v_{3}
and
v_{4}
that was observed in the functional model constructed in
section

4.2
.

Moreover, note that the same cyclic graph
G
also has a non-trivial
p
-separation. In particular notice that
(v_{3}\perp^{d}v_{4}|v_{1},v_{2})_{G}
. It is straightforward to see by applying
definition

19
to the graph
G_{\textsc{tp}}\in\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
that we also have
(v_{3}\perp^{p}v_{4}|v_{1},v_{2})_{G}
, since conditioning additionally on
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
blocks all paths between the remaining vertices and the post-selection vertex
T_{1}
. The soundness of
p
-separation then implies that the conditional independence
(X_{3}\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{4}|X_{1},X_{2})_{\hyperlink{probf}{\mathrm{Pr}}_{G}}
will hold for all valid functional causal models one can define on
G
within our framework.

Finally, applying similar arguments to the cyclic graph
G^{\textup{Neal}}
(
equation

59
) underlying the example of
[
Nea00
]
, where we had the
d
-separation
(v_{4}\perp^{d}v_{5}|v_{2})_{G^{\textup{Neal}}}
, we can see that we would have the
p
-connection
(v_{4}\not\perp^{p}v_{5}|v_{2})_{G^{\textup{Neal}}}
. This is because, just as in the simpler example above, any member of
\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G^{\textup{Neal}})}
will necessarily involve at least one post-selection vertex within the loop between
v_{6}
and
v_{7}
and conditioning on this, the exogenous vertices
v_{4}
and
v_{5}
influencing the loop, will become
d
-connected (and stay that way also when conditioning on
v_{2}
), i.e.,
(v_{4}\not\perp^{d}v_{5}|\{v_{2}\}\cup V_{\textup{post}})_{G^{\textup{Neal}}_{\textsc{tp}}}
will hold for every
G^{\textup{Neal}}_{\textsc{tp}}\in\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G^{\textup{Neal}})}
. This implies the
p
-connection
(v_{4}\not\perp^{p}v_{5}|v_{2})_{G^{\textup{Neal}}}
, which can explain the correlations seen between the outcomes
x_{4}
and
x_{5}
conditioned on
x_{2}
in the uniquely solvable functional model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G^{\textup{Neal}}}
of that example (reviewed in
section

4.2
).

4.5
Links to
\sigma
-separation

In light of the failure of the
d
-separation theorem in cyclic causal models, another generalization of
d
-separation for cyclic graphs, known as
\sigma
-separation, was developed in
[
FM17
]
. This property,

\sigma
-separation was proven to be sound and complete for a class of functional causal models (fCMs) known as modular fCMs or mfCMs
[
FM17
]
8
8

8

These are referred to as modular structural equation models, (mSEM), in
[
FM17
]
.
Modular fCMs correspond to fCMs defined generally on discrete and continuous variables, where the functional dependences possess a stronger version of the unique solvability property.
Although mfCMs are a well-behaved and important subclass of classical causal models with many desirable properties, there do exist well-defined and consistent functional models outside this class for which the
\sigma
-separation theorem (i.e., the soundness and completeness of the property) no longer applies, and cannot account for the correlations there.
In contrast, the
p
-separation theorem proven here applies to all consistent functional causal models, but with the restriction to finite-cardinality variables. More generally, as shown in the companion paper
[
FGV25
]
, the theorem also extends to all consistent cyclic quantum causal models involving finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. Thus the domains of validity of the
\sigma
- and
p
-separation theorems are quite distinct, neither being a subset of the other (non-uniquely solvable and quantum models are excluded in the former while functional models involving infinite-cardinality discrete variables as well as continuous variables are excluded in the latter).

Despite the different approaches backing
\sigma
and
p
-separation, some interesting points of comparison can be made, which suggest new research directions.
We do not discuss the definition of
\sigma
-separation here (for that we refer to
[
FM17
]
), but we consider a particular example often used in the previous literature. Consider the following cyclic graph:

(66)

Applying the definition of
d
-separation, it is easy to confirm that
(v_{1}\perp^{d}v_{3}|v_{2},v_{4})_{G}
and
(v_{2}\perp^{d}v_{4}|v_{1},v_{3})_{G}
hold in this graph. Nevertheless
[
FM17
,
FM18
]
shows that there exist functional causal models on this graph, where the variables associated with the vertices are continuous (take real values,
\mathbb{R}
) which can generate correlations between the variables associated with
v_{1}
and
v_{3}
even when conditioned on the variables of
v_{2}
and
v_{4}
. On the other hand, relative to
\sigma
-separation, denoted as
\perp^{\sigma}
,
(v_{1}\not\perp^{\sigma}v_{3}|v_{2},v_{4})_{G}
and
(v_{2}\not\perp^{\sigma}v_{4}|v_{1},v_{3})_{G}
which can explain the correlations in this case (where
d
-separation fails to do so). In other words, observing such correlations in this graph would certify a correlation gap between
d
and
\sigma
separation.

Interestingly,
p
-separation in this case agrees with
d
-separation rather than
\sigma
-separation, and we can check that we have the
p
-separations
(v_{1}\perp^{p}v_{3}|v_{2},v_{4})_{G}
and
(v_{2}\perp^{p}v_{4}|v_{1},v_{3})_{G}
. This is because, even when introducing post-selection vertices for any number of edges and conditioning on them, the conditioning on the vertices
v_{2}
and
v_{4}
or
v_{1}
and
v_{3}
blocks all the paths between the remaining vertices under consideration. As a consequence of
theorem

20
about the soundness of
p
-separation, it follows that for every causal model on the above graph where the vertices have non-trivial outcome sets, we will have the conditional independencies
(X_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{3}|X_{2},X_{4})_{\hyperlink{probf}{\mathrm{Pr}}_{G}}
and
(X_{2}\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{4}|X_{1},X_{3})_{\hyperlink{probf}{\mathrm{Pr}}_{G}}
. In other words, this implies that a gap between
d
and
\sigma
separation cannot be certified in the above graph using correlations arising in any classical causal model that can be described within our framework. Using the results of
[
FGV25
]
, even allowing for quantum causal models on finite dimensional Hilbert spaces would not allow to certify this gap, as the soundness of
p
-separation extends to the quantum case.

A crucial point to note here is that our framework assumes only finite-cardinality random variables. The previously found distinguishing example between
d
- and
\sigma
-separation involves continuous variables, which are not covered by our formalism.
This observation also highlights that the intuition given by
p
-separation may not immediately generalize to continuous models. Indeed, our method for computing probabilities in cyclic causal models relies on post-selecting on the precise values of certain variables. In the case of continuous variables, this post-selection event is of measure zero, and the associated probability of success would tend to zero
9
9

9

In the quantum case, this discussion is related to the difficulty in defining an analogue of a maximally entangled state between infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces while ensuring a bounded norm for that state.
. In
section

6
, we further discuss possible directions for future research based on such comparisons between graph-separation properties and associated correlations.

5
Solvability and related properties of cyclic functional models

An interesting question arising in the literature is whether the functional dependencies of a given causal models can be jointly satisfied for a choice of value assignment over error vertices. Explicitly, given a valuation
\{u_{v}\in\mathcal{X}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
over the error variables, one considers whether there exists a set
\{x_{v}\in\mathcal{X}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
of values on the vertices such that

x_{v}=f^{v}\Big(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v}\Big)\text{ for all }v\in V

(67)

holds (then the values entail a
consistent solution
), and, eventually, whether this set is unique (this entails a
unique solution
).
For functional models on arbitrary cyclic graphs, the existence of a consistent solution is not guaranteed. However, if we restrict to functional models on
acyclic
graphs, for all valuations of the error variables, a consistent solution that satisfies all functional dependencies always exists and is unique
[
Pea09
]
.
In this section, we aim to characterize the existence and number of solutions of a given finite functional causal model, and discuss how this relates to other properties of the model, such as Markovianity.

5.1
Number of solutions and consistency

We begin by formally defining the number of solutions of a functional causal model over finite-cardinality variables on an arbitrary graph. Then, we review the notion of unique solvability and introduce a new notion of average unique solvability, proving links to other properties. The proofs relative to results of this section are given in
appendix

C
.

Definition 21

(Number of solutions)
.

Consider a functional model on a directed graph
G=(V,E)
,
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
, and a set of value assignments on error variables,
u=\{u_{v}\in\mathcal{U}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
. The number of solutions of the model, for the given value assignment on exogenous vertices, is defined as

\textup{N}_{\mathsf{fCm}}(u)=\sum_{x}\prod_{v\in V}\delta_{f^{v}(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v}),x_{v}},

(68)

where we used the same notation of
definition

1
and the sum is over the value assignments,
x=\{x_{v}\in\mathcal{X}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
.

Notice that the number of solutions of a model is defined independently of the prior distributions
p^{v}
associated to error variables
u_{v}
for
v\in V
in that model. Thus, we introduce the notion of average number of solutions of a functional model which takes into account these distributions.

Definition 22

(Average number of solutions)
.

Consider a functional model on a directed graph
G=(V,E)
,
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
, the average number of solutions of the model is defined as

\overline{\textup{N}}_{\mathsf{fCm}}=\sum_{u}\textup{N}_{\mathsf{fCm}}(u)\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v}),

(69)

where the sum runs over
u=\{u_{v}\in\mathcal{U}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
.

The following proposition connects the probability of successful post-selection (
definition

10
) of a teleportation causal model in
\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
to the average number of solutions of the underlying causal model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
.

Proposition 23

(Average number of solutions and success probability)
.

Consider a functional causal model over a directed graph
G=(V,E)
,
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
. Let
G_{\textsc{tp}}\in\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
be a teleportation graph and
\mathsf{fCm}_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}
the corresponding teleportation causal model constructed from
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
. The post-selection success probability
p_{\textup{s}}
(
definition

10
) of
\mathsf{fCm}_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}
satisfies

p_{\textup{s}}=\left(\prod_{v\in V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})}p_{\textsc{tp}}^{(v)}\right)\overline{\textup{N}}_{\mathsf{fCm}},

(70)

where
V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})
is the set of split vertices of
G_{\textsc{tp}}
and
p_{\textsc{tp}}^{(v)}
is the teleportation probability associated to the classical post-selected teleportation protocol implemented for the split vertex
v\in V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})
.

The previous proposition allows us to characterize inconsistent functional models, i.e., models where
p_{\textup{s}}=0
, in terms of the average number of solutions. This follows immediately from
proposition

23
and from the fact that
p_{\textsc{tp}}>0
by definition of teleportation protocol.

Corollary 24

(Characterization of inconsistent models)
.

A functional causal model on a directed graph
G
,
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
, is inconsistent (
definition

12
) if and only if
\overline{\textup{N}}_{\mathsf{fCm}}=0
.

An interesting subset of functional models is the set of so-called
uniquely solvable
functional models (see also
[
FM17
,
BFPM21
]
.

Definition 25

(Uniquely solvable functional models)
.

A functional model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
on a directed graph
G=(V,E)
is uniquely solvable if for all value assignments on error variables,
u=\{u_{v}\in\mathcal{U}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
, it holds that

\textup{N}_{\mathsf{fCm}}(u)=1.

(71)

In analogy to uniquely solvable models, we can define averagely uniquely solvable models as follows.

Definition 26

(Averagely uniquely solvable models)
.

A functional model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
on a directed graph
G=(V,E)
as in
definition

1
is averagely uniquely solvable if it holds

\overline{\textup{N}}_{\mathsf{fCm}}=1.

(72)

Clearly, every uniquely solvable model is also averagely uniquely solvable. This is proven in the following proposition.

Proposition 27

(Unique solvability implies average unique solvability)
.

A uniquely solvable functional model is also averagely uniquely solvable.

The converse, however, is not true. The examples of the next section show that there are functional models that are only averagely uniquely solvable.

5.2
Examples on solvability

In this section, we first consider acyclic models, then discuss two examples of cyclic functional models and their solvability.

Acyclic functional models.

It is easy to see that acyclic models are uniquely solvable, indeed, given
definition

2
we have

\textup{N}_{\mathsf{fCm}}(u)=\sum_{x}\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x|u)=1,

(73)

where
u=\{u_{v}\in\mathcal{U}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
and the sum runs over
x=\{x_{v}\in\mathcal{X}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
.

(74)

associate the sets
\mathcal{X}_{A}=\mathcal{X}_{B}=\{0,1,2\}
to each vertex as well as the deterministic functions

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=f^{A}(b):=2b\mod{3},

(75)

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=f^{B}(a):=a.

Since the functions are deterministic, the error variables are irrelevant, and we obtain

\textup{N}_{\mathsf{fCm}}=\sum_{a,b\in\{0,1,2\}}\delta_{a,f^{A}(b)}\delta_{b,f^{B}(a)}=\delta_{0,0}\delta_{0,0}=1,

(76)

since the only consistent solution is
a=b=0
.

(77)

Each random variable is taken to be binary, so that
\mathcal{X}_{X_{1}}=\mathcal{X}_{X_{2}}=\{0,1\}
.
We choose a uniform probability distribution associated to the binary error variable of
X_{2}
,
U_{2}
,
p^{2}(0)=p^{2}(1)=1/2
, and for all
i=1,2
we let the function
f^{i}
associated to the vertex
X_{i}
be,
for all
u_{2},x_{i}\in\{0,1\}
.,

\displaystyle x_{1}

\displaystyle=f^{1}(x_{2}):=x_{2},

(78)

\displaystyle x_{2}

\displaystyle=f^{2}(u_{2},x_{1}):=x_{1}\oplus u_{2}.

We have, for
u_{2}=0
,

\left.\begin{aligned} x_{1}&=f^{1}(x_{2})=x_{2}\\
x_{2}&=f^{2}(0,x_{1})=x_{1}\end{aligned}\right\}\implies x_{1}=x_{2}.

(79)

Hence,
\textup{N}_{\mathsf{fCm}}(u_{2}=0)=2
.
For
u_{2}=1
,

\left.\begin{aligned} x_{1}&=f^{1}(x_{2})=x_{2}\\
x_{2}&=f^{2}(1,x_{1})=x_{1}\oplus 1\end{aligned}\right\}\implies x_{1}=x_{2}\neq x_{1}.

(80)

Hence,
\textup{N}_{\mathsf{fCm}}(u_{2}=1)=0
.
The model is not uniquely solvable,
but it is averagely uniquely solvable, since using the given error distribution we obtain

\overline{\textup{N}}_{\mathsf{fCm}}=\frac{1}{2}\textup{N}_{\mathsf{fCm}}(u_{2}=0)+\frac{1}{2}\textup{N}_{\mathsf{fCm}}(u_{2}=1)=1.

(81)

5.3
Linking Markov factorization and average unique solvability

An important property of probability distributions arising in causal models is the Markov property, which captures that the distribution factorizes according to the underlying directed graph. This is defined as follows.

Definition 28

(Markov property in functional causal models)
.

Consider a functional causal model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
over a directed graph
G=(V,E)
. If the joint distribution over observed events
x=\{x_{v}\in\mathcal{X}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
factorizes as in
equation

6
i.e.,

\hyperlink{probf}{\mathrm{Pr}}(x)_{G}=\prod_{v\in V}\mathrm{Pr}^{(v)}\left(x_{v}|\mathrm{Pa}(x_{v})\right)_{G},

(82)

where
\mathrm{Pr}^{(v)}
is the conditional probability distribution specified by
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
for all
v\in V
,
then we say that the model satisfies the Markov property or is probabilistically Markovian.

With this definition, it is immediate to see that all acyclic functional causal models satisfy this Markov property by
definition

2
of their probabilities. Moreover as the probability rule for uniquely solvable models is identical to the acyclic case,
10
10

10

One can see this explicitly in the upcoming
84
.

they also respect this property
[
FM17
,
BFPM21
]
.
However, generic functional models on cyclic graphs are not necessarily Markovian, as there exist examples of non-uniquely solvable models
[
VC22a
]
whose distribution does not factorize as in
definition

28
. Using our framework, we are able to characterize the set of functional models which satisfy the Markov property. The following propositions shows that in the finite cardinality case, Markovianity is satisfied in a strictly larger set of models than the uniquely solvable ones, namely, it is satisfied for all average unique solvable models.
Furthermore, this is the largest set of models satisfying Markovianity.

Corollary 29
.

Given a functional causal model on a directed graph
G
,
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
, the following statements are equivalent:

1.

\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
is probabilistically Markovian;

2.

\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
is averagely uniquely solvable,
\overline{\textup{N}}_{\mathsf{fCm}}=1
;

3.

for every choice of
G_{\textsc{tp}}\in\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
used in the definition of
p_{\textup{s}}
(
definition

10
), the post-selection success probability is

p_{\textup{s}}=\left(\prod_{v\in V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})}p_{\textsc{tp}}^{(v)}\right),

(83)

where
V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})
is the set of split vertices of
G_{\textsc{tp}}
and
p_{\textsc{tp}}^{(v)}
is the teleportation probability associated to the classical post-selected teleportation protocol implemented for the split vertex
v\in V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})
.

The above corollary follows directly from
propositions

23
and
13
. Indeed, it holds that

\displaystyle\hyperlink{probf}{\mathrm{Pr}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{x_{v},f^{v}\left(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v}\right)}}{\sum_{y}\sum_{u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{y_{v},f^{v}\left(y_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v}\right)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{x_{v},f^{v}\left(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v}\right)}}{\overline{\textup{N}}_{\mathsf{fCm}}},

(84)

where the sum runs over
u=\{u_{v}\in\mathcal{U}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
.
The numerator of the above equality yields the Markov property if and only if the constant denominator,
\overline{\textup{N}}_{\mathsf{fCm}}
, is equal to
1
, i.e., if the model is averagely uniquely solvable.

Links between
d
-separation and Markovianity.

In acyclic functional causal models, the probability distribution is given by
definition

2
, which trivially satisfies the Markov property of
definition

28
. For these models we also know that
d
-separation is sound and complete (
theorem

18
). This is not a coincidence, as it is known that in directed acyclic graphs
d
-separation and Markovianity are equivalent to each other, and to another property (called the local Markov property) which states that the variable at each vertex is conditionally independent of its non-descendants given the parents
[
LDLL90
,
GP90
]
. However, in cyclic models this is no longer the case
[
VP22
]
. Firstly, while we have shown that Markovianity in the sense of
definition

28
holds for all averagely uniquely solvable models,
d
-separation soundness fails in this set as shown by the example of the uniquely solvable model given in
[
Nea00
]
(reviewed in
section

4.2
).

Furthermore, consider the graph

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.15pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#5483B0} {{}{}{{

(85)

The local Markov property implies that
v_{2}
is independent of
v_{3}
(which is the only non-descendent which is not a parent of
v_{2}
), conditioned on
\mathrm{Pa}(v_{2})=\{v_{1},v_{4}\}
, that is
{(X_{2}\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{3}|X_{1},X_{4})_{G}}
, where
X_{i}
is the random variable associated to the vertex
v_{i}
.
However, it holds that
(v_{2}\not\perp^{d}v_{3}|v_{1},v_{4})_{G}
, since the path
v_{2}\rightarrow v_{1}\leftarrow v_{3}
is a collider, hence conditioning on
v_{1}

d
-connects
v_{2}
and
v_{3}
allowing the associated variables to be conditionally dependent in a general functional model on this graph. This highlights that in cyclic graphs,
d
-separation, Markovianity (as in
definition

28
) and the local Markov property are not equivalent.

6
Discussion and outlook

We have developed a general framework applicable to all consistent cyclic
functional causal models defined on finite-cardinality variables. The formalism includes both uniquely and non-uniquely solvable models and only excludes pathological models which admit no solutions for all possible valuations of the exogenous vertices
11
11

11

In case the graph contains no exogenous vertices, these would be models admitting no solutions.
.
We applied this framework to solve two open problems, for all (possibly cyclic) causal models describable within this formalism: (1) providing a general and robust method to uniquely fix the probability distribution, while recovering the known probability rule in the uniquely-solvable (and hence acyclic) case, and (2) introducing a new sound and complete graph-separation property, called
p
-separation, for cyclic models, which is equivalent to
d
-separation in the acyclic case. This was achieved by introducing the concept of a classical post-selected teleportation protocol (inspired by an analogous quantum information protocol), which enabled us to map cyclic functional causal models to acyclic ones with post-selection. Moreover, we defined a new class of models, averagely uniquely solvable functional causal models, finding this to be a strict superset of uniquely solvable ones, and the largest set where the previously known probability rule based on Markov factorization relative to the underlying graph is recovered.

There are several interesting avenues for future investigations and we discuss some of them below.

1.

Causal discovery algorithms:
These algorithms aim to ascertain the underlying causal structure (graph) from observable data, or a set of possible graphs compatible with the data. In acyclic graphs, the
d
-separation theorem plays a crucial role in such algorithms (see
[
SZ16
]
).
Causal discovery algorithms in the cyclic case have been proposed for different subsets of models, e.g., for modular functional causal models based on
\sigma
-separation theorem
[
MC20
]
. As we discussed in
section

4
, in the finite-cardinality case, our framework and the
p
-separation theorem apply to more general models which include non-uniquely solvable ones.
A natural next step would be to study whether robust causal discovery algorithms based on
p
-separation can be developed, as this would be applicable to all consistent finite-cardinality (possibly cyclic) functional causal models.

2.

Causal compatibility problems:
This involves determining whether a given distribution could arise from any classical causal model (here, a functional causal model) on a given causal structure. Again, as this involves relating the causal structure and observable distributions, it is also intimately linked to the
d
-separation theorem. This problem has applications for classical causal inference on the one hand, because focussing on classical causal explanations, incompatibility with a graph would allow to rule out that graph as an explanation
[
Pea09
]
. On the other hand, if a distribution generated by a quantum causal model on the same graph
12
12

12

That is, using quantum states and measurements wired together as suggested by the graph. See
[
FGV25
]
for details.
is classically incompatible as above, this would certify a quantum-classical gap in that causal structure. Bell’s seminal theorem
[
Bel64
]
provides the first instance of an acyclic graph with such a gap, which is verifiable experimentally and has lead to several quantum information processing applications (see
[
BCP+14
]
for a review on Bell nonlocality, including relevant experiments and applications).
Our contributions enable to extend such studies to cyclic graphs.
In particular, since our framework maps cyclic causal modeling problems to acyclic causal modeling with post-selection, it is likely that existing techniques developed for solving acyclic causal compatibility problems can be ported to cyclic causal compatibility problems. For instance, it would be interesting to consider if the inflation technique for causal compatibility
[
WSF19
]
would generalize to cyclic causal models, using these ideas.

3.

Correlation gaps between
d
-,
\sigma
- and
p
-separation and unfaithful models:

In
section

4
, we demonstrated that in the graph of
equation

66
, there exists a
d
-separation relation that aligns with
p
-separation but not with
\sigma
-separation. Combined with our
p
-separation theorem, this implies that for all functional causal models within our framework, the resulting correlations would be unfaithful or fine-tuned relative to
\sigma
-separation (as conditional independence holds, yet there is
\sigma
-connection).
13
13

13

A causal model is unfaithful (or fine-tuned) relative to a graph-separation property if the model entails a conditional independence between three sets of variables whose vertices are connected in the graph according to the property. If this is never the case, the model is faithful relative to the given graph-separation property.
This finding further suggests that within this graph, a correlation gap between
d
- and
\sigma
-separation cannot be established using functional models in our framework. However, there do exist continuous variable models, not captured within our framework, that are
\sigma
-faithful for this graph and certify such a gap
[
FM17
,
FM18
]
. Consequently, our results, together with these prior examples, also point to a correlation gap between finite-cardinality and infinite-cardinality or continuous functional causal models for the graph in
equation

66
14
14

14

In conjunction with the results of
[
FGV25
]
, this also highlights a gap between all finite-dimensional quantum causal models and continuous variable functional causal models in the graph of
equation

66
.
.

Similarly, we identified another situation—the graph in
equation

56
—where the vertices
v_{3}
and
v_{4}
are both
d
- and
\sigma
-separated but not
p
-separated. The soundness of
\sigma
-separation in all modular functional causal models
[
FM17
]
implies that such models will be unfaithful relative to
p
-separation. Furthermore, it indicates that a correlation gap between
d
- and
p
-separation may not be certifiable through such models for this particular graph.

Whether these curious observations generalize to arbitrary directed graphs remains an open question. This opens new directions for future exploration into correlation gaps between constraints imposed by different graph separation properties and among various classes of causal models on a given directed graph. Additionally, in the acyclic case, it is well-established that the set of unfaithful causal models on a graph (i.e., where there is
d
-connection and yet conditional independence) forms a measure-zero set
[
Pea09
,
Spi05
]
. The above discussion sheds light on the open problem regarding the generalization of this result for cyclic graphs, for (un)faithfulness defined relative to the distinct properties of
\sigma
- or
p
-separation (both of which reduce to
d
-separation in the acyclic case).

4.

Links between graph separation properties and Markovianity:

As discussed in
section

5.3
, the notion of Markovianity and the soundness of
d
-separation are equivalent for functional models on acyclic graphs. However, this equivalence no longer holds for models on cyclic graphs. It would be interesting to characterize the class of cyclic models in our framework for which the equivalence is recovered. In particular, although we found that the Markov factorization is recovered for all averagely uniquely solvable models, the example of
[
Nea00
]
(a uniquely solvable model) demonstrates that the
d
-separation theorem cannot be recovered for all such models. Therefore, tighter conditions would be required for the equivalence, and it would also be fruitful to consider other types of Markov properties such as the local Markov property, corresponding to conditional independence between a vertex and its non-descendants given its parents (which is also equivalent to
d
-separation properties in the acyclic case). In addition, relations between Markov properties and
p
-separation are yet to be explored as well as possible uses of
p
-separation in extending previous studies on Markov equivalence classes
[
Ric97
,
CM23
]
.

5.

Characterising classical processes with “indefinite causal order”:
We note a striking similarity between our results of
section

5.3
(regarding average unique solvability) and those in the literature on so-called indefinite causal order or higher-order processes
[
OCB12
,
CDPV13
,
BW16b
]
studied in the quantum information community. Specifically, classical processes in such formalisms are higher-order transformations from
functions to functions, where the set of input functions could be composed together in a manner where one cannot identify a definite acyclic ordering between them
[
BW16b
]
. It was shown in
[
BW16a
]
that valid classical processes are characterized by having exactly one fixed point, and, by allowing probabilistic mixtures of such processes, the requirement becomes that of having one fixed point of average. There as well, one generally considers functions on finite alphabets, as in our functional causal modeling framework.

It is not difficult to see that each such classical process together with the input functions on which it acts specifies a (possibly cyclic) functional causal model that is valid in our framework
15
15

15

This is also implied by the our results in the accompanying paper
[
FGV25
]
for general quantum processes.
. This highlights a general link between average uniquely solvable functional causal models defined here and the class of classical higher-order processes. Thus, it indicates future avenues to investigate open problems on the characterization of higher-order processes by linking them to problems regarding solvability and Markov properties of cyclic functional models, and to study these in mutual synergy within a common formalism.

6.

Incorporating interventions:
We have focussed on properties of correlations obtainable through passive observation in cyclic fCMs. Another natural extension of this work would be to incorporate active interventions within this framework and study the consistency and solvability properties of fCMs under interventions. The class of allowed interventions is also expected to play a role. Commonly, do-interventions are considered which fix a variable to a certain value independently of its parents
[
Pea09
,
Spi05
]
. The above-mentioned links to higher-order processes also suggests more general interventions where the functional dependences may be replaced without “cutting off” a vertex from its parents.

7.

Generalization to infinite-cardinality random variables:
Our framework is applicable to functional causal models associated with finite-cardinality random variables. The generalization to infinite-cardinality models (including both discrete and continuous random variables) is not immediate because it would require post-selecting on a measure-zero event. Therefore, the question on whether and how the probability rule and
p
-separation can be generalized to (possibly non-uniquely solvable) infinite-cardinality models remains open. Whether the discrete with infinite-cardinality vs continuous case impacts this generalization is also open. In particular, the discussions comparing
p
and
\sigma
separation
[
FM17
]
above and in
section

4
suggest that for
p
-separation as defined here, soundness could fail in continuous variable functional causal models. It would be interesting to investigate possible extensions of the classical post-selected teleportation protocol introduced here to the infinite-cardinality case, to explore modifications to
p
-separation that may arise in that case.

]

Proof.

Assume that
p_{\textsc{tp}}
depends on the probability being teleported, i.e.,
p_{\textsc{tp}}=p_{\textsc{tp}}\big(p^{A}\big)
, and for all
c\in\mathcal{X}_{C}
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathcal{X}_{A}\\
b\in\mathcal{X}_{B}\end{subarray}}p^{A}(a)\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{f}}(a,b,c),1}{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{B}(b){\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{C}(c)=p_{\textsc{tp}}\big(p^{A}\big)p^{A}(c),

(86)

and assume that there exist two distinct probabilities
p^{A}
and
{p^{\prime}}^{A}
such that
p_{\textsc{tp}}(p^{A})\neq p_{\textsc{tp}}({p^{\prime}}^{A})
. Consider a mixture of the two, i.e.,
\lambda p^{A}+(1-\lambda){p^{\prime}}^{A}
for some
\lambda\in[0,1]
, and teleport it:

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b}\left[\lambda p^{A}(a)+(1-\lambda){p^{\prime}}^{A}(a)\right]\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{f}}(a,b,c),1}{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{B}(b){\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{C}(c)

(87)

\displaystyle=p_{\textsc{tp}}\left(\lambda p^{A}+(1-\lambda){p^{\prime}}^{A}\right)\left[\lambda p^{A}(c)+(1-\lambda){p^{\prime}}^{A}(c)\right].

(88)

By linearity on the left-hand side of the equation, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a,b}\left[\lambda p^{A}(a)+(1-\lambda)p^{A^{\prime}}(a)\right]\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{f}}(a,b,c),1}{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{B}(b){\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{C}(c)

\displaystyle=\lambda\sum_{a,b}p^{A}(a)\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{f}}(a,b,c),1}{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{B}(b){\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{C}(c)+(1-\lambda)\sum_{a,b}p^{A^{\prime}}(a)\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{f}}(a,b,c),1}{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{B}(b){\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{C}(c)

\displaystyle=\lambda p_{\textsc{tp}}\big(p^{A}\big)p^{A}(c)+(1-\lambda)p_{\textsc{tp}}\big(p^{A^{\prime}}\big)p^{A^{\prime}}(c).

(89)

Combining
equations

88
and
89
we have

\displaystyle\lambda p_{\textsc{tp}}\left(p^{A}\right)p^{A}(c)+(1-\lambda)p_{\textsc{tp}}\left({p^{\prime}}^{A}\right)p^{A^{\prime}}(c)

\displaystyle=p_{\textsc{tp}}\left(\lambda p^{A}+(1-\lambda){p^{\prime}}^{A}\right)\left[\lambda p^{A}(c)+(1-\lambda){p^{\prime}}^{A}(c)\right],

(90)

and marginalising over
c
,

\lambda p_{\textsc{tp}}\big(p^{A}\big)+(1-\lambda)p_{\textsc{tp}}\big({p^{\prime}}^{A}\big)=p_{\textsc{tp}}\left(\lambda p^{A}+(1-\lambda){p^{\prime}}^{A}\right).

(91)

Inserting the last equation in
90
, we get a quadratic expression in
\lambda
which has to hold for all
\lambda\in[0,1]
:

\left(p_{\textsc{tp}}\big(p^{A}\big)-p_{\textsc{tp}}\big({p^{\prime}}^{A}\big)\right)\left(p^{A}(c)-{p^{\prime}}^{A}(c)\right)\lambda^{2}+(\dots)\lambda+(\dots)=0.

(92)

This implies that that the term before
\lambda^{2}
should vanish for all
c\in\mathcal{X}_{C}
. This contradicts our assumption that
p^{A}\neq{p^{\prime}}^{A}
and
p_{\textsc{tp}}(p^{A})\neq p_{\textsc{tp}}({p^{\prime}}^{A})
.
∎

Lemma 30
.

The uniform prior post-selected teleportation protocol (
definition

6
) is a valid classical post-selected teleportation protocol with
p_{\textsc{tp}}=1/|\mathcal{X}_{A}|
.

Proof.

We have, for all distribution
p^{A}
over
\mathcal{X}_{A}
,

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{X}_{A}}p^{A}(a)\delta_{\delta_{a,c},1}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{X}_{A}|}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{X}_{A}}p^{A}(a)\delta_{a,c}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{X}_{A}|}=\frac{p^{A}(c)}{|\mathcal{X}_{A}|}.\qed

Proof.

In the first step of
definition

7
, the graph
G^{\prime}
is acyclic by definition.
The second step introduces pre- and post-selection vertices,
R_{v}
and
T_{v}
for each
v\in V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})
, and associated edges according to
equation

26
.
To show that this step preserves acyclicity, notice that a directed graph is acyclic if and only if it can be drawn on a page with all directed edges oriented from bottom to top of the page
16
16

16

This representation is equivalent to a Hasse diagram for partially ordered sets, recognising the fact that every directed acyclic graph induces a partial order and vice versa.
.
Since
G^{\prime}
is acyclic, we can represent it on a page through such a diagram.
We now add the pre- and post-selection vertices to this diagram by drawing all the
R_{v}
at the bottom of the page, below all other vertices, and all the
T_{v}
at the top of the page, above all the other vertices.
Since all preselection vertices
R_{v}
only have outgoing edges and all post-selection vertices
T_{v}
only have incoming arrows, both of which will be oriented from bottom to top in our diagram, it follows that
G_{\textsc{tp}}
is acyclic.
∎

See
[use

Proof.

In what follows we will use the following result: given a functional model (
definition

1
) on an acyclic graph
\tilde{G}=(\tilde{V},\tilde{E})
and a global observed event

(95)

where we denoted

and defined

v\notin\mathrm{Ch}(v_{0})\end{subarray}}p^{v}(x_{v})\delta_{f^{v}(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v}),x_{v}}.

(96)

Notice that

is just the marginal
\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(\bar{x})_{\tilde{G}}
where

.
With this in mind, we first proceed with proving the result in a special case.

Proof in a special case.

. Let

.

Denoting with

associated to the causal model

.
As this is an acyclic causal model by construction, the probability is immediately given by applying the acyclic probability rule of

2
to this functional model.
As argued above, this can be factorized
17
17

17

This factorization corresponds to considering the product of all probabilities and delta functions of the functional model

but leaving the multiplication along the split vertex
v_{0}
as the last step.
as

\displaystyle\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x,t)_{G_{1}}=\sum_{u}p(u,\bar{x},t)\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Ch}(v_{0})}\delta_{f^{v}(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v}),x_{v}}p^{v}(u_{v})

(98)

where

was defined as prescribed in
equation

96
18
18

18

Note that for

, the joint observed event includes the post-selections, i.e., it is
x\cup t
. Since
v_{0}
is not split in
G_{1}
,
T\not\in\mathrm{Ch}(G_{1})
for all

.

Let
({\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{f}},{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{B},{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{C})
be the classical post-selected teleportation protocol which implements the splitting of

and

For all,

\sum_{b\in\mathcal{X}_{B}}\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{f}}(a,b,c),1}{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{B}(b){\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{C}(c)=p_{\textsc{tp}}\delta_{a,c}.

(99)

Using this equality, we can rewrite

\begin{split}\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x,t)_{G_{1}}&=\sum_{u}p(u,\bar{x},t)\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Ch}(v_{0})}\delta_{f^{v}(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v}),x_{v}}p^{v}(u_{v})\\
&=\sum_{u}p(u,\bar{x},t)\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Ch}(v_{0})}\delta_{f^{v}(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)\setminus v_{0}},x_{0},u_{v}),x_{v}}p^{v}(u_{v})\sum_{r_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{v_{0}}}\delta_{x_{0},r_{0}}\\
&=\sum_{u}p(u,\bar{x},t)\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Ch}(v_{0})}p^{v}(u_{v})\sum_{r_{0}}\delta_{f^{v}(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)\setminus v_{0}},r_{0},u_{v}),x_{v}}\delta_{x_{0},r_{0}}\\
&=\frac{1}{p_{\textsc{tp}}}\sum_{u}p(u,\bar{x},t)\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Ch}(v_{0})}p^{v}(u_{v})\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\sum_{r_{0},b}\delta_{f^{v}(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)\setminus v_{0}},r_{0},u_{v}),x_{v}}\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{f}}(x_{0},b,r_{0}),1}{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{B}(b){\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{C}(r_{0})\\
&=\frac{1}{p_{\textsc{tp}}}\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x,t,t_{0}=1)_{G_{2}},\end{split}

(100)

where in

we separated the dependency on the parent node
v_{0}
as
f^{v}(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)\setminus v_{0}},x_{0},u_{v})
.
In the above steps, we have used that dividing by
p_{\textsc{tp}}
is allowed as

by definition and
lemma

5
between the third to fourth step above.
In the last equation, we used that
\mathsf{fCm}_{G_{1}}
and
\mathsf{fCm}_{G_{2}}
can be chosen to have the same associated

(to see this, one may restrict all the post-selected teleportation protocols to be implemented as in

14
).

We can now relate the success probabilities

and

respectively of the functional model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G_{1}}
on the classical teleportation graph

.

\displaystyle p_{\textup{s}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x,t=1)_{G_{1}}

(101)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p_{\textsc{tp}}}\sum_{x}\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x,t=1,t_{0}=1)_{G_{2}}

(102)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p_{\textsc{tp}}}p_{\textup{s}}^{(2)},

(103)

where we denoted with

the event

runs over all

.

We see that the two success probabilities differ by a multiplicative constant (the success probability

of the post-selected teleportation protocol defined by

for the split vertex

), and hence,

is zero.
Thus, the probabilities

are defined if and only if the probabilities

are defined. In this case, we can relate them as follows:

\begin{split}\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x|t=1)_{G_{1}}&=\frac{\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x,t=1)_{G_{1}}}{p_{\textup{s}}^{(1)}}\\
&=\frac{p_{\textsc{tp}}}{p_{\textsc{tp}}}\frac{\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x,t=1,t_{0}=1)_{G_{2}}}{p_{\textup{s}}^{(2)}}\\
&=\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x|t=1,t_{0}=1)_{G_{2}},\end{split}

(104)

which completes the proof in this case.

Proof in the general case.
Consider two general elements
G_{1},G_{2}
of
\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
.
We can prove the general statement by first noticing that the repeated application of the above argument can be used to prove that the probabilities of both
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are equivalent to those of
G_{0}
, where the latter corresponds to the graph where all the vertices have been split (i.e.,
V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{0})=V
, where
G=(V,E)
).
The result follows by transitivity. ∎

]

Proof.

We have shown that the probability rule is independent on the set of split vertices
V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})
, hence we can consider the element
G_{0}=(V_{0},E_{0})
of
\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
where
V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})=V
, i.e., all vertices are split and associated with pre- and post-selection vertices and edges.
Therefore, the set of vertices of
G_{0}
is
V_{0}=V\cup\{R_{v},T_{v}\}_{v\in V}
, i.e., for each vertex in
G
there is a pre- and post-selection pair of vertices in
G_{0}
.
For
v\in V
we denote with
({\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{f}}^{v},{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}^{v}_{B},{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}^{v}_{C})
the post-selected teleportation protocol associated to the corresponding pre- and post-selection pair.

, we have (

2
)

\displaystyle\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x,\{t_{v}=1\}_{v\in V})_{G_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r}\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x,r,\{t_{v}=1\}_{v\in V})_{G_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r,b,u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{x_{v},f^{v}(r_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v})}\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{f}}^{v}(x_{v},b_{v},r_{v}),1}{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{B}^{v}(b_{v}){\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{C}^{v}(r_{v})

\displaystyle\hskip-9.38945pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{lem: copy property aug}}}{{=}}\sum_{r,u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{x_{v},f^{v}(r_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v})}p_{\textsc{tp}}^{(v)}\;\delta_{x_{v},r_{v}}

\displaystyle=p_{\textsc{tp}}^{\text{tot}}\sum_{u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{x_{v},f^{v}(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v})}

where

and

. Therefore,

\displaystyle\hyperlink{probf}{\mathrm{Pr}}(x)_{G}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x,\{t_{v}=1\}_{v\in V})_{G_{0}}}{\sum_{y}\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(y,\{t_{v}=1\}_{v\in V})_{G_{0}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{\textsc{tp}}^{\text{tot}}\sum_{u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{x_{v},f^{v}(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v})}}{p_{\textsc{tp}}^{\text{tot}}\sum_{y,u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{y_{v},f^{v}(y_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v})}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{x_{v},f^{v}(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v})}}{\sum_{y,u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{y_{v},f^{v}(y_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v})}}.\qed

Appendix B
Proofs of
section

4

We provide the proof of
theorem

20
.
See
[use
cref
not
ref
]

Proof.

(Soundness)

From
definition

19
, we have
(V_{1}\perp^{p}V_{2}|V_{3})_{G}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \exists G_{\textsc{tp}}\in\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
such that
(V_{1}\perp^{d}V_{2}|V_{3}\cup V_{\textup{post}})_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}
, where
V_{\textup{post}}
is the set of all post-selection vertices in the chosen classical teleportation graph
G_{\textsc{tp}}
.
From
theorem

18
, it then follows that the conditional independence
(X_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{2}|X_{3}\cup V_{\textup{post}})_{(\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}})_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}}
holds since
G_{\textsc{tp}}
is a directed acyclic graph (
lemma

8
). Finally, by
definition

12
, the probability distribution associated with a functional model on the possibly cyclic graph
G
is given by the corresponding probability computed in any representative teleportation graph
G_{\textsc{tp}}\in\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
with an additional conditioning on the associated post-selection vertices
V_{\textup{post}}
. Recall from
proposition

11
that this distribution is the same, independently of which representative
G_{\textsc{tp}}\in\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
is chosen. Therefore, using
definition

12
, we know that
(X_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{2}|X_{3}\cup V_{\textup{post}})_{(\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}})_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}}
is equivalent to
(X_{1}\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{2}|X_{3})_{\hyperlink{probf}{\mathrm{Pr}}_{G}}
by
definition

19
, which completes the proof.

(Completeness)
We begin by making an important observation.

Observation 1:
Consider a directed graph
G:=(V,E)
and an arbitrary subgraph
G^{\prime}:=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
of
G
with
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
and
E^{\prime}\subseteq E
.
Then any functional causal model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G^{\prime}}
(
definition

1
) on the subgraph trivially induces a functional causal model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
on the original graph
G
where both models assign the same probabilities to the values
x^{\prime}:=\{x_{v}\}_{v\in V^{\prime}}
of variables associated to
V^{\prime}
, i.e.,
\mathrm{Pr}_{G^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})=\mathrm{Pr}_{G}(x^{\prime})
. Explicitly,
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
is constructed from the given
\mathsf{fCm}_{G^{\prime}}
by including the same error distribution
p^{v}(u_{v})
and same function
f^{v}
to each
v\in V^{\prime}
in both models, in
G
, these functions ignore any dependencies on parents of
v
associated with edges in
E\backslash E^{\prime}
. For all remaining vertices,
v\in V\backslash V^{\prime}
, the error distributions
p^{v}(u_{v})
can be arbitrary and the functions set
x_{v}=u_{v}
. As the models
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
and
\mathsf{fCm}_{G^{\prime}}
are exactly the same for the common vertices
V^{\prime}
, it is immediate that the probabilities for
x^{\prime}
are identical.

Now suppose we have a
p
-connection
(V_{1}\not\perp^{p}V_{2}|V_{3})_{G}
, and consider two cases based on whether or not the corresponding
d
-connection holds. We will establish the existence of a causal model with the corresponding conditional dependence separately in each case.

Proof when
d
- and
p
-connections coincide
, i.e.,

We now define a generic functional model applicable to any graph as follows.

Definition 31

(A functional model on an arbitrary graph)
.

Associate a binary outcome
x_{v}\in\mathcal{X}_{v}:=\{0,1\}
to each vertex
v
of the given graph. Every exogenous vertex
v\in V_{\textup{ex}}
is associated with a uniform distribution
p^{v}(x_{v}=0)=p^{v}(x_{v}=1)=\frac{1}{2}
and every endogenous vertex
v\in V_{\textup{end}}
is associated with a function
x_{v}=f^{v}\big(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)}\big):=\bigoplus\limits_{u\in\mathrm{Pa}(v)}x_{u}
. Here, we have chosen a functional model where the functions deterministically relate each variable to its parental variables in the graph, and have thus applied the simplification allowed by the remark of
section

2
.

This fully specifies the functional model
(
definition

1
). In particular, applying the above definition to the acyclic graph

we will denote the resulting functional model as

21

21

Observation 1
here implies that, in case the parent set of a vertex does not coincide between

and

refers to the parent set of
G_{\textsc{tp}}^{\prime}
.

.

The functional dependences in the above model encode that the outcome value for each vertex

v
. In particular, note that if

.

(115)

Explicitly, recalling that
G_{\textsc{tp}}^{\prime}
is a directed acyclic graph we can directly compute the joint probability distribution of
\mathsf{fCm}_{G_{\textsc{tp}}^{\prime}}
(
definition

2
). Then, the functional dependence
x_{T}\oplus x_{v_{1}}=x_{T^{\prime}}\oplus x_{v_{2}}
, which holds when
x_{C}=0
, translates to the conditional dependence

(\bar{X}_{1}\not\perp\!\!\!\perp\bar{X}_{2}|T=0,T^{\prime}=0,C=0)_{\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}_{G_{\textsc{tp}}^{\prime}}},

(116)

where
\bar{X}_{i}
denotes the random variable associated to the vertex
v_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Note that the remaining vertices are causally disconnected in
G_{\textsc{tp}}
(in the sense that are not involved in any functional dependencies, except trivially on their error variable), by construction of the way the causal model on the subgraph
G_{\textsc{tp}}^{\prime}
is extended to a causal model on
G_{\textsc{tp}}
in
Observation 1
, and conditioning on the outcomes of such vertices cannot uncorrelate the remaining outcomes in an acyclic functional model.
Using this, we see that
equation

116

implies that
(\bar{X}_{1}\not\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{2}|V_{3}\cup\{T=0\}_{T\in V_{\textup{post}}})_{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}}
also holds using
definition

17
, where we recall that
X_{i}
is the set of variables associated to the vertex set
V_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
This trivially implies, by inclusion, that

(X_{1}\not\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{2}|V_{3}\cup\{T=0\}_{T\in V_{\textup{post}}})_{\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}}
holds.

Finally, to link the constructed model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}
on the teleportation graph
G_{\textsc{tp}}\in\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
of
G
to a causal model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
on the original (possibly cyclic) graph
G
, it is important to note that our mapping from cyclic functional models to acyclic functional models with post-selection relies on a particular form of post-selection at the post-selection vertices
V_{\textup{post}}
. The post-selections must form a post-selected teleportation protocol (
definition

3
) for each pair of pre and post-selection vertices in
equation

111
.

Notice that the graph
G
differs from
G_{\textsc{tp}}
specifically by replacing each structure of the form
equation

111
with a directed edge

v
u

and thus,
G
contains no pre and post-selection vertices, only observed vertices

v

. By post-selecting on
\{t_{T}=0\}_{T\in V_{\textup{post}}}
(i.e.,
0
outcome on all post-selection vertices) in the causal model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}
defined on
G_{\textsc{tp}}
, we induce a causal model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
on
G
where one replaces
r_{R}
(outcome of a pre-selection vertex) appearing in any functional dependence with the outcome
x_{v}
for the corresponding vertex
v
(which has an edge to the same
T
as
R
, as in
equation

111
). This way all functional dependences are expressed only in terms of the vertices of
G
, and
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
is a fully specified functional model on
G
.

This establishes that the constructed causal model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}
on the acyclic teleportation graph
G_{\textsc{tp}}
of
G
induces a functional model
\mathsf{fCm}_{G}
on the possibly cyclic graph
G
where
\hyperlink{probf}{\mathrm{Pr}}(x)_{G}=\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x|\{t_{T}=0\}_{T\in V_{\textup{post}}})_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}
. Here the
t_{T}=0
post-selection plays the same role as the post-selection on
x_{T}=1
in
definition

12
and
x=\{x_{v}\}_{v\in V}
is the set of all observed outcomes of the graph.

Therefore the the conditional dependence
(X_{1}\not\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{2}|X_{3}\cup\{T=0\}_{T\in V_{\textup{post}}})_{\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}_{G_{\textsc{tp}}}}
established above for the acyclic model on the teleportation graph
G_{\textsc{tp}}
implies
(X_{1}\not\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{2}|X_{3})_{\hyperlink{probf}{\mathrm{Pr}}_{G}}
, as required.

Proof for the general case:
We now consider the case of more general paths that can ensure the
d
-connections in
equation

110
, as compared to the paths in
equation

112
,
equation

113
,
equation

114
. Consider the first
d
-connection
(v_{1}\not\perp^{d}T|V_{3})_{G_{\textsc{tp}}^{\prime}}
of
equation

110
. In the simple case of
equation

112
, we argued that for the correlations between outcomes in our functional model, we could equivalently consider
v_{1}
as a direct cause of
T
as in
equation

115
. This used the fact that the
d
-connecting path between
v_{1}
and
T
in
equation

112
ensures equal outcomes for all intervening vertices in the path that are not in the conditioning set
V_{3}
.
It is easy to see that this property holds generically for our causal model, independently of the particular
d
-connecting path
equation

112
, if we condition on
x_{v}=0
for every
v
that features in this
d
-connecting path where
v
or a descendant of it belongs to
V_{3}
.

We argue this through examples for simplicity, but the argument generalizes in a straightforward manner.
Consider the direct cause graph

v_{1}
\tilde{v}

, and the common cause graph

v_{1}
\tilde{u}
v

where
\tilde{u}
in the latter case does not belong to
V_{3}
(otherwise,
v_{1}
and
\tilde{v}
would be
d
-separated by conditioning on
V_{3}
).
In both these cases, the causal model from
definition

31
ensures
x_{v_{1}}=x_{\tilde{v}}
(
=x_{\tilde{u}}
).
Now consider the collider graph

v_{1}
\tilde{u}
\tilde{v}

where
\tilde{u}\in V_{3}
(so that
v_{1}
and
\tilde{v}
are
d
-connected given
V_{3}
).
Our causal model ensures that
x_{\tilde{u}}=x_{v_{1}}\oplus x_{\tilde{v}}
i.e., if we post-select on
x_{\tilde{u}}=0
, we have equality
x_{v_{1}}=x_{\tilde{v}}
.
Even if
\tilde{u}\not\in V_{3}
but
\tilde{u}
is a descendant of
u^{\prime}\in V_{3}
, this means we have a directed path from
\tilde{u}
to
u^{\prime}
and conditioning on
x_{u^{\prime}}=0
would give
x_{\tilde{u}}=0
and thereby
x_{v_{1}}=x_{\tilde{v}}
. Note that as we apply the functional model defined in
definition

31
to the subgraph
G_{\textsc{tp}}^{\prime}
comprised only of edges in a relevant
d
-connecting path, any such colliders
\tilde{u}
will only have at most two children (two children if it is itself a collider and one child if it is a descendant of a collider) in
G_{\textsc{tp}}^{\prime}
even if this vertex has more parents in
G_{\textsc{tp}}
.

In a similar manner, we can consider the second
d
-connection
(v_{2}\not\perp^{d}T^{\prime}|V_{3})_{G_{\textsc{tp}}^{\prime}}
of
equation

110
and obtain that for the purpose of our arguments, we can equivalently consider
v_{2}
as a direct cause of
T^{\prime}
as in
equation

115
.

Finally for the third
d
-connection
(T\not\perp^{d}T^{\prime}|V_{3})_{G_{\textsc{tp}}^{\prime}}
of
equation

110
, we can apply a similar argument as well. In
equation

114
, the central observed vertex
C
acts as a collider. We may also have a situation where it acts as part of a chain.

C
R
T
T^{\prime}

(117)

In this case, for this to be the
d
-connecting path for the third condition of
equation

110
, we must have
C\not\in V_{3}
.
The causal model of
definition

31
for this case entails
x_{C}=x_{R}
, i.e., this is again equivalent to
T
and
T^{\prime}
sharing the same common cause
R
as in
equation

115
.

Another case is where
C
is a common cause of
T
and
T^{\prime}
(then
C\not\in V_{3}
), here we already have the structure of
equation

115
(it does not matter for our arguments whether the common cause is
C
or
R
, the causal model behaves in the same way). More generally,
T
and
T^{\prime}
could be
d
-connected through paths that have additional intervening vertices
T^{\prime\prime}\in V_{\textup{post}}
which are conditioned on. These arguments straightforwardly generalize to that case, post-selecting on the
0
value of all such vertices again reduces our arguments to a situation of the form of
equation

115
.

Therefore all the steps following
equation

115
also apply to the general case, allowing us to establish the required conditional independence
(X_{1}\not\perp\!\!\!\perp X_{2}|X_{3})_{\hyperlink{probf}{\mathrm{Pr}}_{G}}
.

This completes the proof. ∎

Appendix C
Proofs of
section

5

See
[use
cref
not
ref
]

Proof.

Let us denote with

p_{\textup{s}}^{G_{\textsc{tp}}}=\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}\left(\{t_{T}=1\}_{T\in V_{\textup{post}}}\right)_{G_{\textsc{tp}}},

(118)

the success probability relative to
G_{\textsc{tp}}\in\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
, with post-selection set
V_{\textup{post}}
. We first prove the statement for a specific teleportation graph.

Consider the teleportation graph
G_{0}\in\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
where
V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{0})=V
, i.e., all vertices are split and associated with pre- and post-selection vertices and edges as in
definition

9
.
Therefore, the set of vertices of
G_{0}
is
V_{0}=V\cup\{R_{v},T_{v}\}_{v\in V}
.
For each
v\in V
we denote with
({\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{f}}^{v},{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}^{v}_{B},{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}^{v}_{C})
the augmented post-selected teleportation protocol associated to the corresponding pre and post-selection pair.

Since all vertices are split, for each vertex
v\in V
the variable associated to
v
,
x_{v}
is the value associated to such vertex, obtained through
f^{v}
, and the variable associated to the reconsigning pre-selection vertex
R_{v}
,
r_{v}
, acts as input of the functions
f^{v^{\prime}}
where
v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}(v)
. Thus, denoting with
t_{v}\in\{0,1\}
the values associated to
T_{v}
and with
r:=\{r_{v}\in\mathcal{X}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
the values associated to the preselection vertices
\{R_{v}\}_{v\in V}
, we have (
definition

2
)

\displaystyle\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(\{t_{v}=1\}_{v\in V})_{G_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r,x}\hyperlink{probfacyc}{{\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathrm{acyc}}}}(x,r,\{t_{v}=1\}_{v\in V})_{G_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r,b,x,u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{x_{v},f^{v}(r_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v})}\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{f}}^{v}(x_{v},b_{v},r_{v}),1}{\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{B}^{v}(b_{v}){\color[rgb]{0.7891,0.0781,0.1133}{\mathcal{P}}}_{C}^{v}(r_{v})

\displaystyle\hskip-7.68236pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{lem: copy property aug}}}{{=}}\sum_{r,x,u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{x_{v},f^{v}(r_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v})}p_{\textsc{tp}}^{(v)}\delta_{x_{v},r_{v}}

\displaystyle=p_{\textsc{tp}}^{\text{tot}}\sum_{x,u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\delta_{x_{v},f^{v}(x_{\mathrm{Pa}(v)},u_{v})}

\displaystyle=p_{\textsc{tp}}^{\text{tot}}\sum_{u}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\textup{N}_{\mathsf{fCm}}(u)

\displaystyle=p_{\textsc{tp}}^{\text{tot}}\overline{\textup{N}}_{\mathsf{fCm}},

(119)

where
p_{\textsc{tp}}^{\text{tot}}=\prod_{v\in V}p_{\textsc{tp}}^{(v)}
and
p_{\textsc{tp}}^{(v)}
is the probability of successful post-selection of the implementation chosen for the vertex
v
.

Consider an arbitrary teleportation graph
G_{\textsc{tp}}\in\mathbb{G}_{\textsc{tp}}{(G)}
with
V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})\subseteq V
. In the proof of
proposition

11
we related the success probabilities of two teleportation graphs. By applying recursively
equation

101
we get

p_{\textup{s}}^{(G_{\textsc{tp}})}=\left(\frac{p_{\textsc{tp}}^{\text{tot}}}{\prod_{v\notin V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})}p_{\textsc{tp}}^{(v)}}\right)\overline{\textup{N}}_{\mathsf{fCm}}=\left(\prod_{v\in V_{\textsc{s}}(G_{\textsc{tp}})}p_{\textsc{tp}}^{(v)}\right)\overline{\textup{N}}_{\mathsf{fCm}},

(120)

which completes the proof.
∎

not
ref
]

Proof.

Consider a uniquely solvable functional model: it holds that

\overline{\textup{N}}_{\mathsf{fCm}}=\sum_{x}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})\textup{N}_{\mathsf{fCm}}(u)=\sum_{x}\prod_{v\in V}p^{v}(u_{v})=1,

(121)

where the sum runs over
x=\{x_{v}\in\mathcal{X}_{v}\}_{v\in V}
.
∎
